This is an eigenvalue problem. \emph{The polarizations that can travel unchanged are the eigenvectors of $\varepsilon_{r\perp}$, and their refractive indices squared are its eigenvalues.} A medium is \newterm{birefringent} when the two eigenvalues differ. Away from a resonance the friction $\gamma$ can be dropped, the matrix is then Hermitian in both cases below, and the indices are real; a nonzero $\gamma$ makes them complex, which is absorption, and absorption that differs between the two eigenvectors is called dichroism.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9]
  \tikzset{stage/.style={faint}, flow/.style={->,softgray,thick}}
  \foreach \y/\lab in {0/{crystal\\(case A)},-2.7/{magnetized\\plasma\\(case B)}}{
    \node[font=\small,anchor=east,align=right] at (-1.1,\y) {\lab};
    \draw[stage] (-0.85,\y-0.85) rectangle (0.85,\y+0.85);
    \draw[very thick,formblue] ($(0,\y)+(45:0.6)$) -- ($(0,\y)+(225:0.6)$);
    \draw[flow] (1.05,\y) -- (1.75,\y);
    \draw[flow] (7.35,\y) -- (8.05,\y);
    \draw[stage] (8.25,\y-0.85) rectangle (9.95,\y+0.85);
  }
  \draw[stage] (1.95,-0.85) rectangle (4.45,0.85);
  \draw[stage] (4.85,-0.85) rectangle (7.15,0.85);
  \draw[very thick,tangentgreen] (2.6,0) -- (3.8,0);
  \node[font=\small] at (3.2,-0.55) {index $n_x$};
  \draw[very thick,fibreorange] (6.0,-0.6) -- (6.0,0.6);
  \node[font=\small,anchor=west] at (6.1,-0.55) {$n_y$};
  \node[font=\small] at (4.65,0) {$+$};
  \draw[very thick,formblue,rotate around={45:(9.1,0)}] (9.1,0) ellipse (0.62 and 0.3);
  \draw[stage] (1.95,-3.55) rectangle (4.45,-1.85);
  \draw[stage] (4.85,-3.55) rectangle (7.15,-1.85);
  \draw[very thick,tangentgreen,->] (3.2,-2.7) ++(0:0.5) arc (0:300:0.5);
  \node[font=\small] at (3.2,-3.35) {$\bm e_+$, index $n_+$};
  \draw[very thick,fibreorange,->] (6.0,-2.7) ++(0:0.5) arc (0:-300:0.5);
  \node[font=\small] at (6.0,-3.35) {$\bm e_-$, index $n_-$};
  \node[font=\small] at (4.65,-2.7) {$+$};
  \draw[very thick,formblue] ($(9.1,-2.7)+(15:0.6)$) -- ($(9.1,-2.7)+(195:0.6)$);
  \draw[softgray,dotted] ($(9.1,-2.7)+(45:0.6)$) -- ($(9.1,-2.7)+(225:0.6)$);
  \node[font=\small] at (0,1.15) {enters};
  \node[font=\small,align=center] at (4.55,1.3) {splits into the two polarizations that keep their shape,\\each travelling with its own index};
  \node[font=\small] at (9.1,1.15) {leaves};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{What a birefringent medium does, in three stages. A wave enters linearly polarized at $45^\circ$. Inside, it is a sum of the medium's two eigen-polarizations, which travel at different speeds and drift apart in phase. In a crystal these are two straight lines, along $x$ and $y$, and the recombined wave is an ellipse. In a magnetized plasma they are the two circular polarizations, and the recombined wave is again a straight line, turned by half the phase difference (the dotted line is the entering direction).}
\label{T1c:fig:lorentz-scheme}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Case A: a crystal.} Let the springs have different stiffness along $x$ and $y$, $\Omega_0^2=\mathrm{diag}(\omega_{0x}^2,\omega_{0y}^2,\omega_{0z}^2)$, with no magnetic field and no friction. Every matrix in \eqref{T1c:eq:lorentz-chi} is then diagonal, and so is
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon_{r\perp}=\begin{pmatrix}1+\dfrac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_{0x}^2-\omega^2}&0\\[2mm]0&1+\dfrac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_{0y}^2-\omega^2}\end{pmatrix}.
\label{T1c:eq:crystal}
\end{equation}
Its eigenvectors are $\hat{\bm x}$ and $\hat{\bm y}$, with indices $n_x^2$ and $n_y^2$ equal to the two diagonal entries; they differ when $\omega_{0x}\ne\omega_{0y}$. This is \newterm{linear birefringence}: a wave polarized along $x$ or along $y$ crosses the crystal unchanged. \emph{What happens to a wave at $45^\circ$?} At the entrance it is $\bm E=\frac{E_0}{\sqrt2}(\hat{\bm x}+\hat{\bm y})$. Each part travels with its own wavenumber $k=n\omega/c$, so after a length $L$
\begin{align}
\bm E(L)&\eqby{1}\frac{E_0}{\sqrt2}\bigl(\hat{\bm x}\,e^{in_x\omega L/c}+\hat{\bm y}\,e^{in_y\omega L/c}\bigr)
\eqby{2}e^{in_x\omega L/c}\,\frac{E_0}{\sqrt2}\bigl(\hat{\bm x}+\hat{\bm y}\,e^{i\delta}\bigr),
\qquad \delta\equiv(n_y-n_x)\frac{\omega L}{c},
\nonumber\\
Q&\eqby{3}\tfrac12E_0^2-\tfrac12E_0^2=0,
\qquad
U\eqby{4}2\cdot\tfrac12E_0^2\cos(0-\delta)=E_0^2\cos\delta .
\label{T1c:eq:case-a}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item Each eigenvector picks up its own phase $kL=n\omega L/c$ and keeps its direction.
  \item Take out the phase of the $x$ part, which is common to the whole wave and invisible in the Stokes numbers.
  \item The definition \eqref{T1c:eq:stokes} with $a_1=a_2=E_0/\sqrt2$.
  \item The same with the phase difference $\theta_1-\theta_2=-\delta$.
\end{explain}
The tip of the real field traces $E_x\propto\cos\omega t$, $E_y\propto\cos(\omega t-\delta)$: a line for $\delta=0$, an ellipse with its axes along the two diagonals, a circle at $\delta=\pi/2$, and a line along the other diagonal at $\delta=\pi$ (\cref{T1c:fig:lorentz}(a)). $Q$ stays zero and the linear polarization $\abs U=E_0^2\abs{\cos\delta}$ shrinks and comes back, while the circular part $V$, set aside since \cref{T1c:sec:stokes}, grows as $\sin\delta$. The stick is never turned; the light becomes partly circular. Even the plate with $\delta=\pi$ that sends the $45^\circ$ line to $-45^\circ$ is not a rotation: it sends a stick at $\psi$ from the $x$ axis to $-\psi$, so a stick at $10^\circ$ goes to $-10^\circ$ and one at $30^\circ$ to $-30^\circ$. That is a mirror in the $x$ axis, not the same turn for every stick.

\paragraph{Case B: electrons in a magnetic field.} Now let the springs be equally stiff in every direction, $\Omega_0^2=\omega_0^2\Id$, and put a steady field $\bm B_0=B_0\hat{\bm z}$ along the direction of the wave. The magnetic force on the moving charge, with $\dot{\bm r}=-i\omega\bm r$ and $\bm r\times\hat{\bm z}=(y,-x,0)$, is $q\dot{\bm r}\times\bm B_0=-i\omega qB_0\,(y,-x,0)$. It pushes $x$ with $y$ and $y$ with $-x$. The transverse rows of \eqref{T1c:eq:lorentz-eom} become
\begin{equation}
M\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix}=\frac qm\begin{pmatrix}E_x\\E_y\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&i\omega\omega_c\\-i\omega\omega_c&a\end{pmatrix},
\qquad a\equiv\omega_0^2-\omega^2-i\omega\gamma,\qquad \omega_c\equiv\frac{qB_0}m,
\label{T1c:eq:lorentz-b}
\end{equation}
where $\omega_c$ is the \newterm{cyclotron frequency}, the rate at which the charge would circle the field line, with a sign set by the charge and the direction of the field. The magnetic field has added off-diagonal entries with opposite signs, an antisymmetric part, which a crystal cannot produce because its spring matrix is symmetric. \emph{Which vectors does $M$ leave in their own direction?} Try the helicity vectors $\bm e_\pm=(\hat{\bm x}\pm i\hat{\bm y})/\sqrt2$ of \eqref{T1c:eq:helicity}:
\begin{align}
M\bm e_\pm&\eqby{1}\frac1{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix}a+i\omega\omega_c(\pm i)\\-i\omega\omega_c+a(\pm i)\end{pmatrix}
\eqby{2}\frac1{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix}a\mp\omega\omega_c\\\pm i(a\mp\omega\omega_c)\end{pmatrix}
\eqby{3}(a\mp\omega\omega_c)\,\bm e_\pm,
\nonumber\\
n_\pm^2&\eqby{4}1+\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_0^2-\omega^2\mp\omega\omega_c}\qquad(\gamma=0).
\label{T1c:eq:case-b}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item Multiply the matrix into the column $(1,\pm i)/\sqrt2$.
  \item In the first entry $i\cdot(\pm i)=\mp1$; in the second, $-i\omega\omega_c=\pm i\,(\mp\omega\omega_c)$ because $(\pm i)(\mp1)=-i$.
  \item Both entries are $(a\mp\omega\omega_c)$ times the entries of $\bm e_\pm$.
  \item $\chi=\omega_p^2M^{-1}$ by \eqref{T1c:eq:lorentz-chi}; the inverse of a matrix has the same eigenvectors and the inverse eigenvalues, so $\varepsilon_{r\perp}=\Id+\chi$ has eigenvalues $1+\omega_p^2/(a\mp\omega\omega_c)$.
\end{explain}
The two waves that keep their shape are the two helicities, with different indices $n_+\ne n_-$ as soon as $\omega_c\ne0$. This is \newterm{circular birefringence}. \emph{What happens to a linear polarization?} By \eqref{T1c:eq:helicity}, a stick at angle $\psi$ is the equal mixture $(e^{-i\psi}\bm e_++e^{i\psi}\bm e_-)/\sqrt2$. After a length $L$ each helicity has picked up its own phase $\phi_\pm=n_\pm\omega L/c$, and with $\Delta\equiv\phi_+-\phi_-$ and $\bar\phi\equiv\frac12(\phi_++\phi_-)$,
\begin{align}
\frac{e^{-i\psi}e^{i\phi_+}\bm e_++e^{i\psi}e^{i\phi_-}\bm e_-}{\sqrt2}
&\eqby{1}e^{i\bar\phi}\,\frac{e^{-i(\psi-\Delta/2)}\bm e_++e^{i(\psi-\Delta/2)}\bm e_-}{\sqrt2}
\eqby{2}e^{i\bar\phi}\,\bigl[\cos(\psi-\tfrac\Delta2)\,\hat{\bm x}+\sin(\psi-\tfrac\Delta2)\,\hat{\bm y}\bigr].
\label{T1c:eq:case-b-turn}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item Write $\phi_\pm=\bar\phi\pm\Delta/2$ and take out the common phase $e^{i\bar\phi}$.
  \item \eqref{T1c:eq:helicity} with $\psi$ replaced by $\psi-\Delta/2$.
\end{explain}
The wave leaves still linearly polarized, with the same strength, and its plane turned by $-\Delta/2$: half the phase difference of the two helicities, as found in \cref{T1c:sec:what-twists}, and the same turn for every starting angle $\psi$ (\cref{T1c:fig:lorentz}(b)). The sense of the turn is set by the sign of $\omega_c$, that is by the sign of the charge and the direction of the field. This is a rotation, a twist of every stick by the same angle, and it is the rule \eqref{T1c:eq:twist} with $\beta=-\Delta/2$.

\emph{How does the angle depend on frequency?} Take free electrons in an ionized gas: no spring, $\omega_0=0$, charge $q=-e$, and no friction. Then $n_\pm^2=1-\omega_p^2/[\omega(\omega\pm\omega_c)]$, and the gas is transparent only above the plasma frequency. For radio and microwave light crossing interstellar or intergalactic gas the plasma and cyclotron frequencies are in the kilohertz range or below, far beneath the gigahertz of the light, so we expand in the small ratios $\omega_p/\omega$ and $\omega_c/\omega$:
\begin{align}
n_\pm&\eqby{1}\Bigl[1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}\Bigl(1\pm\frac{\omega_c}\omega\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr]^{1/2}
\eqby{2}1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{2\omega^2}\Bigl(1\mp\frac{\omega_c}{\omega}\Bigr),
\nonumber\\
\Delta&\eqby{3}(n_+-n_-)\frac{\omega L}c
\eqby{4}\frac{\omega_p^2\omega_c}{\omega^3}\,\frac{\omega L}c
=\frac{\omega_p^2\,\omega_c\,L}{c\,\omega^2}
\eqby{5}\frac{\omega_p^2\,\omega_c\,L}{4\pi^2c^3}\,\lambda^2 .
\label{T1c:eq:faraday}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item Take $\omega^2$ out of $\omega(\omega\pm\omega_c)$ and the square root of $n_\pm^2$.
  \item To first order, $(1\pm x)^{-1}\approx1\mp x$ and $(1-y)^{1/2}\approx1-y/2$; products of the two small ratios beyond $\omega_p^2\omega_c/\omega^3$ are dropped.
  \item The definition of $\Delta$ with $\phi_\pm=n_\pm\omega L/c$.
  \item In the difference of the two indices the terms $1-\omega_p^2/2\omega^2$ cancel; what is left is $+\omega_p^2\omega_c/2\omega^3$ in $n_+$ and $-\omega_p^2\omega_c/2\omega^3$ in $n_-$, which differ by $\omega_p^2\omega_c/\omega^3$.
  \item $\omega=2\pi c/\lambda$, so $1/\omega^2=\lambda^2/(4\pi^2c^2)$.
\end{explain}
The index difference falls as $\omega^{-3}$, the path in wavelengths grows as $\omega$, and the phase difference, hence the angle $\abs\Delta/2$, grows as $\lambda^2$. That is the law of \newterm{Faraday rotation}. Inserting $\omega_p^2=n_ee^2/(\varepsilon_0m_e)$ for $n_e$ electrons per unit volume and $\abs{\omega_c}=eB_\parallel/m_e$, and adding up the contributions of a gas whose density and field change along the path, the angle is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\abs\Delta}2=\mathrm{RM}\,\lambda^2,
\qquad
\mathrm{RM}=\frac{e^3}{8\pi^2\varepsilon_0m_e^2c^3}\int n_eB_\parallel\,\dd l
=\TOneCRMconst\,\mathrm{rad\,m^{-2}}\int\frac{n_e}{\mathrm{cm}^{-3}}\,\frac{B_\parallel}{\mu\mathrm G}\,\frac{\dd l}{\mathrm{pc}},
\label{T1c:eq:rm}
\end{equation}
the standard rotation measure, which is what \cite{KosowskyLoeb1996} evaluate for a primordial field. The $\lambda^2$ law is the high-frequency limit. \Cref{T1c:fig:lorentz}(c) compares it with the exact indices of \eqref{T1c:eq:case-b} for a plasma with $\omega_c=0.2\,\omega_p$: at ten times the plasma frequency the exact angle exceeds the law by $\TOneCFarDevTen\%$, at three times by $\TOneCFarDevThree\%$, and near the plasma frequency the law fails. For the CMB, more than a million times above the plasma frequency of the gas it crosses, the law is exact for all practical purposes.

\paragraph{Case C: cosmic birefringence.} The medium is now empty space filled with the slowly changing pseudoscalar field $\phi$ of \eqref{T1c:eq:beta-phi}. With the coupling $\frac14g_{\phi\gamma}\phi F_{\mu\nu}\tilde F^{\mu\nu}$, Maxwell's equations again have the two helicities as the waves that keep their shape, and again give them different phases. The difference is how that phase depends on frequency. For a field changing in time at the rate $\dot\phi$, the two helicities with the same wavenumber have frequencies that differ by $g_{\phi\gamma}\dot\phi$, the same amount for every wavelength \cite{CarrollFieldJackiw1990,Carroll1998}. Equivalently, at one frequency their wavenumbers differ by a fixed amount. Over the journey from last scattering the phase difference adds up to $g_{\phi\gamma}(\phi_{\mathrm{obs}}-\phi_{\mathrm{LSS}})$, and by \eqref{T1c:eq:case-b-turn} the plane turns by half of it, which is the twist $\beta$ of \eqref{T1c:eq:beta-phi} (its sign depends on the conventions for $g_{\phi\gamma}$ and for the angle). In Faraday rotation the splitting of the two indices, $n_+-n_-\propto\omega^{-3}$, falls faster than the path in wavelengths grows, and the angle goes as $\lambda^2$; here the splitting of the wavenumbers is a constant, $n_+-n_-\propto\omega^{-1}$, which exactly cancels the growth of the path in wavelengths, and the angle is the same at every frequency. This is the difference between the Faraday row and the birefringence row of the table in \cref{T1c:sec:table}, a $\lambda^2$ law against a constant, and it is the reason the colour test of \cref{T1c:fig:frequency}(b) can separate them.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/chT1/lorentz_birefringence.pdf}
\caption{Linear against circular birefringence. A wave enters linearly polarized at $45^\circ$ (dotted). (a) In a crystal, after a phase difference $\delta$ between the $x$ and $y$ parts, the tip of the electric field traces an ellipse, then a circle, then the line along the other diagonal; the axis of the ellipse never turns. (b) With the two helicities as the travelling waves, a phase difference $\Delta$ leaves the light linearly polarized and turns the line by $\Delta/2$. (c) The angle of turn against the square of the wavelength, each normalised to its value at a reference wavelength: Faraday rotation in a cold magnetized plasma from the exact Lorentz-model indices (orange), its high-frequency $\lambda^2$ law (dashed), and cosmic birefringence, the same at every wavelength (green). The exact curve leaves the $\lambda^2$ law only as the frequency approaches the plasma frequency.}
\label{T1c:fig:lorentz}
\end{figure}

\emph{Birefringence is not dispersion.} Birefringence means two different eigenvalues at one frequency: two polarizations travel at two speeds. Dispersion means one eigenvalue that changes with frequency: different colours travel at different speeds. Because $\chi$ in \eqref{T1c:eq:lorentz-chi} depends on $\omega$, the Lorentz model has both, and the Faraday law needs both: the two indices differ, and their difference changes with frequency. Dispersion alone, such as $n^2=1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$ in a plasma without a magnetic field, delays a pulse but leaves every polarization as it was, because both eigenvalues move together.

\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.6cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
medium & what makes the two indices differ & waves that keep their shape & a $45^\circ$ line becomes\\\midrule
crystal & springs of different stiffness & lines along $x$, $y$ & an ellipse, not turned\\
magnetized plasma & the force $q\,\dot{\bm r}\times\bm B_0$ & the two helicities & a line turned by $\propto\lambda^2$\\

\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\subsection{The Galaxy as a reference: the Minami--Komatsu relation}
\label{T1c:sec:mk}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  box/.style={draw=accent,rounded corners=2pt,fill=ideabg,align=center,font=\small,minimum height=0.95cm,inner sep=3pt},
  arr/.style={->,thick}]
  \node[box,minimum width=2.6cm] (lss) at (0,1.1) {last scattering\\ CMB: $E$, no $B$};
  \node[box,minimum width=2.6cm] (gal) at (6.0,-0.6) {our Galaxy\\ dust, synchrotron};
  \node[box,minimum width=2.4cm] (tel) at (10.2,0.3) {detectors\\ angle error $\alpha$};
  \node[box,minimum width=2.1cm,fill=warnbg,draw=bdryred] (map) at (13.4,0.3) {maps\\ $Q$, $U$};
  \draw[arr,tangentgreen] (lss.east) -- (tel.west|-0,0.55);
  \draw[arr,fibreorange] (gal.east) -- (tel.west|-0,0.05);
  \draw[arr,black] (tel) -- (map);
  \node[font=\small,tangentgreen,align=center] at (6.0,2.15) {CMB light: twist $\beta$ over $13.8$ Gyr, then $\alpha$; total $\alpha+\beta$};
  \node[font=\small,fibreorange,align=center] at (6.0,-1.75) {Galactic light: no twist on the way, then $\alpha$; total $\alpha$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The door frame of the microwave sky. CMB light (green) is twisted by $\beta$ on its long journey and then by the detector angle error $\alpha$; light from the Galaxy (orange) travels too short a distance to be twisted by $\beta$ and receives only $\alpha$. The maps contain both, so the twists of the two components can be measured separately.}
\label{T1c:fig:doorframe}
\end{figure}

\emph{The observed spectra.} A map at one frequency contains the CMB, the Galactic foreground (``fg''), and noise. By \cref{T1c:fig:doorframe}, the foreground is twisted by $\alpha$ and the CMB by $\alpha+\beta$, so by \eqref{T1c:eq:rot-eb} applied to each,
\begin{equation}
E^{\mathrm o}_{\ell m}+iB^{\mathrm o}_{\ell m}=e^{2i\alpha}\bigl(E^{\mathrm{fg}}_{\ell m}+iB^{\mathrm{fg}}_{\ell m}\bigr)+e^{2i(\alpha+\beta)}\bigl(E^{\mathrm{CMB}}_{\ell m}+iB^{\mathrm{CMB}}_{\ell m}\bigr)+\text{noise}.
\label{T1c:eq:mk-alm}
\end{equation}
Assume, as \cite{Minami2019,MinamiKomatsu2020} do in their baseline, that neither the foreground nor the CMB has an $EB$ of its own, and that the CMB, the foreground and the noise are uncorrelated, so the cross terms between them vanish on average. Each component then contributes to the averaged spectra exactly as in \cref{T1c:sec:rot-spectra}, with its own angle. Writing $\Delta^{X}_\ell\equiv C^{EE,X}_\ell-C^{BB,X}_\ell$ for the component $X$, and leaving out the noise, which has $EE=BB$ and no $EB$,
\begin{align}
C^{EB,\mathrm o}_\ell&\eqby{1}\tfrac12\sin4\alpha\,\Delta^{\mathrm{fg}}_\ell+\tfrac12\sin(4\alpha+4\beta)\,\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell,
\nonumber\\
C^{EE,\mathrm o}_\ell-C^{BB,\mathrm o}_\ell&\eqby{2}\cos4\alpha\,\Delta^{\mathrm{fg}}_\ell+\cos(4\alpha+4\beta)\,\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell .
\label{T1c:eq:mk-spectra}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item \eqref{T1c:eq:rot-ebsp} for each component, with the angle $\alpha$ for the foreground and $\alpha+\beta$ for the CMB.
  \item $C^{EE\prime}-C^{BB\prime}=\cos4\beta\,(C^{EE}-C^{BB})$ from \cref{T1c:sec:rot-read}, again with each component's own angle; the noise has $C^{EE}=C^{BB}$ and drops out.
\end{explain}
\emph{Removing the unknown foreground.} We do not know $\Delta^{\mathrm{fg}}_\ell$; modelling the Galaxy is exactly what we want to avoid. But it appears in both lines with known coefficients, so it can be eliminated:
\begin{align}
C^{EB,\mathrm o}_\ell-\tfrac12\tan4\alpha\,\bigl(C^{EE,\mathrm o}_\ell-C^{BB,\mathrm o}_\ell\bigr)
&\eqby{1}\tfrac12\bigl[\sin(4\alpha+4\beta)-\tan4\alpha\cos(4\alpha+4\beta)\bigr]\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell
\nonumber\\
&\eqby{2}\frac{\sin(4\alpha+4\beta)\cos4\alpha-\sin4\alpha\cos(4\alpha+4\beta)}{2\cos4\alpha}\,\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell
\nonumber\\
&\eqby{3}\frac{\sin4\beta}{2\cos4\alpha}\,\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell .
\label{T1c:eq:mk}
\end{align}
\begin{explain}
  \item Insert both lines of \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-spectra}; the foreground terms are $\frac12\sin4\alpha\,\Delta^{\mathrm{fg}}-\frac12\tan4\alpha\cos4\alpha\,\Delta^{\mathrm{fg}}=0$, since $\tan4\alpha\cos4\alpha=\sin4\alpha$.
  \item Put both terms over the common denominator $\cos4\alpha$.
  \item The subtraction formula $\sin(A+B)\cos A-\sin A\cos(A+B)=\sin B$ with $A=4\alpha$, $B=4\beta$.
\end{explain}
Rearranged, this is the relation of \cite{Minami2019} (their eq.~9 without the noise and intrinsic-$EB$ terms):
\begin{equation}
C^{EB,\mathrm o}_\ell=\frac{\tan4\alpha}{2}\bigl(C^{EE,\mathrm o}_\ell-C^{BB,\mathrm o}_\ell\bigr)+\frac{\sin4\beta}{2\cos4\alpha}\bigl(C^{EE,\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell-C^{BB,\mathrm{CMB}}_\ell\bigr).
\label{T1c:eq:mk-final}
\end{equation}
Read it in words. The first term uses the \emph{observed} $EE-BB$ of the map, foreground and all, and its coefficient depends only on $\alpha$: everything in the map has been twisted by $\alpha$. The second term uses the \emph{theoretical} CMB spectra from the $\Lambda$CDM fit, and its coefficient carries $\beta$: only the CMB has the extra twist. Where the foreground is negligible, $C^{EE,\mathrm o}-C^{BB,\mathrm o}\approx\cos(4\alpha+4\beta)\Delta^{\mathrm{CMB}}$ has the same shape as the second template and the two terms cannot be separated; that is the degeneracy of \cref{T1c:sec:rot-alpha} again. Where the foreground contributes, the observed $EE-BB$ has a different shape in $\ell$ (a power law added to the acoustic peaks) and a different size in each frequency channel, and the two coefficients are separately measurable. With no foreground at all and $\beta=0$, \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-final} reduces to the sky-by-sky relation $\widehat C^{EB}=\frac12\tan4\alpha(\widehat C^{EE}-\widehat C^{BB})$ verified on simulations in \cref{T1c:sec:rot-sims}.

\emph{How well can it work?} \Cref{T1c:fig:fisher} gives a size, from a Fisher forecast in \nolinkurl{code/chT1/09_alpha_beta_fisher.py}. The set-up is one frequency channel covering $70\%$ of the sky with white noise of $50\,\mu$K-arcmin and a $5'$ beam, $51\le\ell\le1500$, the CMB fiducial, and Planck's dust over $71\%$ of the sky \cite{Planck2018XI}; the data are the $EB$ spectrum \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-spectra}, with the cosmic-variance error of each $\widehat C^{EB}_\ell$, $[C^{EE}_\ell C^{BB}_\ell/(2\ell+1)f_{\mathrm{sky}}]^{1/2}$ with total spectra, as in \cref{ch:10}. With the CMB alone the Fisher matrix is singular: only the sum is measured, $\sigma(\alpha+\beta)=\TOneCSigSum^\circ$, the band in the figure. At $143\,$GHz the dust is faint: it hardly disturbs the CMB, which still fixes $\sigma(\alpha+\beta)=\TOneCSigSumOneFortyThree^\circ$, but it anchors $\alpha$ only weakly, and the band closes into a long, thin ellipse with $\sigma(\beta)=\TOneCSigBOneFortyThree^\circ$. At $353\,$GHz, where the dust is $\TOneCDustRatioFish$ times brighter in power than at $143\,$GHz, the dust anchors $\alpha$ well, $\sigma(\alpha)=\TOneCSigAThreeFiftyThree^\circ$, but over $70\%$ of the sky its $EE$ and $BB$ exceed those of the CMB at every multipole used, so it also swamps the CMB's $EB$ with chance scatter: the sum is known only to $\TOneCSigSumThreeFiftyThree^\circ$, and $\sigma(\beta)=\TOneCSigBThreeFiftyThree^\circ$. One channel faces a trade-off, too little dust to calibrate or so much that it blurs the CMB. In both cases $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are strongly anti-correlated (correlation coefficients $\TOneCRhoOneFortyThree$ and $\TOneCRhoThreeFiftyThree$), because $\alpha+\beta$ is better measured than $\beta$ itself, and $\beta=(\alpha+\beta)-\alpha$. The real analyses combine several channels, each with its own $\alpha_\nu$ and a single $\beta$, which is how \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020} reach $\sigma(\beta)=0.14^\circ$; their posterior shows the same anti-correlation. The forecast assumes the dust spectra are known; the measurement does not need them, because \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-final} uses the observed $EE-BB$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth]{figures/chT1/alpha_beta_fisher.pdf}
\caption{Fisher forecast for the detector angle $\alpha$ and the birefringence angle $\beta$ from the $EB$ spectrum of one channel. With the CMB alone only $\alpha+\beta$ is measured (shaded band, $1\sigma$). Adding the dust of the same map, twisted by $\alpha$ only, closes the band into an ellipse ($1\sigma$). At $143\,$GHz, where the dust is faint, the ellipse is long and stays inside the band: the CMB still fixes the sum, and the dust pins $\alpha$ only weakly. At $353\,$GHz the bright dust pins $\alpha$ tightly, so the ellipse is short in $\alpha$, but the same dust adds scatter to the CMB's $EB$, so the ellipse is wider than the band and $\beta$ is still uncertain by about a third of a degree.}
\label{T1c:fig:fisher}
\end{figure}

\emph{When the door frame is crooked.} The method assumes the Galaxy has no $EB$ of its own. Suppose instead the dust has an intrinsic $EB$, and write it, as \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020} do, as if the dust were twisted at the source by an angle $\gamma$: $C^{EB,\mathrm{dust}}_\ell=\frac12\sin4\gamma\,\Delta^{\mathrm{dust}}_\ell$. Then the dust in the map is twisted by $\alpha+\gamma$ in total, and
\begin{equation}
\hat\alpha\eqby{1}\alpha+\gamma,\qquad
\hat\alpha+\hat\beta\eqby{2}\alpha+\beta
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\hat\beta\eqby{3}\beta-\gamma .
\label{T1c:eq:gamma}
\end{equation}
\begin{explain}
  \item The method reads the total twist of the foreground as the detector angle.
  \item The CMB still fixes the sum of the detector angle and its own twist, which the method splits as $\hat\alpha+\hat\beta$.
  \item Subtract the first equation from the second.
\end{explain}
The estimate of $\beta$ is off by exactly the dust's own handedness. Since the dust $TB$ and $TE$ are both positive, the misalignment picture of \cref{T1c:sec:galaxy} predicts $\gamma>0$, so an analysis that assumes $\gamma=0$ would underestimate $\beta$ \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020}; but the size and even the sign of the dust $EB$ can change with the sky area \cite{Clark2021}.

\subsection{What has been measured, and why it is not yet a detection}
\label{T1c:sec:not-yet}

\emph{The measurements.} With the detector angles fixed at their ground calibration, the Planck team found an angle of $0.31^\circ\pm0.05^\circ$ (statistical) $\pm0.28^\circ$ (systematic), where the second error is the calibration uncertainty of $\alpha$ \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020}. Applying the relation \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-final} to the Planck 2018 maps at $100$, $143$, $217$ and $353\,$GHz, fitting one $\beta$ and four $\alpha_\nu$, \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020} found
\begin{equation}
\beta=0.35^\circ\pm0.14^\circ\quad(68\%),
\end{equation}
which excludes zero at $99.2\%$ confidence, a $2.4\sigma$ result, with the calibration uncertainty no longer in the error. Repeating the analysis on the reprocessed Planck data (PR4) gave $\beta=0.30^\circ\pm0.11^\circ$ for nearly the full sky, with values that decrease as the Galactic mask is enlarged \cite{DiegoPalazuelos2022}. Adding WMAP and the Planck low-frequency channels, $23$ to $353\,$GHz, gave
\begin{equation}
\beta=0.342^{\circ\,+0.094^\circ}_{\ \,-0.091^\circ}\quad(68\%),
\end{equation}
excluding zero at $99.987\%$ confidence, $3.6\sigma$, with no evidence that $\beta$ depends on frequency \cite{EskiltKomatsu2022}. The independence of frequency argues against Faraday rotation, whose angle would change by a factor $(353/23)^2\approx240$ across that range.

\emph{Why is it not yet a detection?} Treat it as an inference problem, in the order used throughout the book. \emph{The event:} an $EB$ (and $TB$) with the shape of a twist, at the level of three to four standard deviations, after the detector angles were fitted using the Galaxy. \emph{What could have produced it?} Four candidates.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Chance.} With a $3.6\sigma$ result, a fluctuation of the noise and of the cosmic variance is unlikely, but particle physics and cosmology reserve the word ``discovery'' for $5\sigma$, because many analyses are performed and systematic errors are often underestimated (\cref{ch:04}).
\item \emph{A crooked door frame.} An intrinsic dust $EB$ shifts the estimate to $\beta-\gamma$ \eqref{T1c:eq:gamma}. Its size is not known well enough: the dust $EB$ predicted from filament misalignment is of the same order as the effect \cite{Clark2021}, and the dependence of $\beta$ on the mask found by \cite{DiegoPalazuelos2022} is what a foreground effect would produce. Those authors chose not to assign cosmological significance to the value until the foreground polarization is better understood.
\item \emph{The instrument.} Leakage from temperature to polarization and beam effects can create $EB$ and $TB$ (\cref{T1c:sec:instrument}). The analyses correct for them with beam models and test the corrections on simulations \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020}, but the corrections are themselves models.
\item \emph{New physics.} A twist \eqref{T1c:eq:beta-phi} by a pseudoscalar field, $g_{\phi\gamma}\Delta\phi$ of about $10^{-2}$ radians \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020}.
\end{enumerate}
Only the last is interesting, and only the last is grey, uniform over the sky and independent of the mask. A detection therefore needs evidence that removes the others: experiments with an independent calibration of $\alpha$ (artificial polarized sources, or well-measured astrophysical ones), a better model of the dust $EB$ from its correlation with $TB$ and $TE$ \cite{Clark2021}, and agreement between instruments with different detectors and scan strategies.

\begin{inpractice}[How the measurement is made]
The published analyses work with the cross-spectra of maps that share no noise: the two half-mission maps of each frequency channel, in all combinations of channels, so that noise bias drops out \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020}. The spectra are measured on the masked sky with a pseudo-$C_\ell$ code (NaMaster) that corrects for the mask, which otherwise leaks $E$ into $B$ and mixes multipoles (the MASTER method of \cref{ch:08}). Equation \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-final}, written for every pair of channels, is turned into a Gaussian likelihood whose covariance is estimated from the observed spectra, and $\alpha_\nu$, $\beta$ are sampled with an MCMC (\cref{ch:09}). The pipeline is validated on end-to-end simulations with no twist, which must return $\beta$ consistent with zero. Our forecast replaces all of this with one channel, a known dust spectrum and a Fisher matrix; it gives the sizes, not the result. \Cref{ch:13} measures the twist on the Planck SMICA maps, where only the sum $\alpha+\beta$ is accessible because a component-separated map has had its Galaxy removed.
\end{inpractice}

One chain of reasoning runs from the sticks to the published angle. Polarization is a field of headless sticks; its split into $E$ and $B$ is a split by behaviour in a mirror; ordinary physics leaves the parity-odd spectra at zero; a uniform twist moves power from $E$ to $B$ and, unlike lensing, leaves a hand behind in $EB$ and $TB$; and the twist of the instrument is told from the twist of the sky only by a component, the Galaxy, that sees one of them and not the other.

\begin{recap}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em,itemsep=1pt]
\item The polarization stick is $Q+iU=Pe^{2i\psi}$; a twist by $\beta$ multiplies it by $e^{2i\beta}$, and turning the axes by $\alpha$ multiplies it by $e^{-2i\alpha}$.
\item $E$ and $B$ are $Q$ and $U$ read in axes aligned with each wavevector; $E$ is mirror symmetric, $B$ changes sign, so a mirror-symmetric universe has $EB=TB=0$.
\item Scalar perturbations make only $E$; gravitational waves make $E$ and $B$.
\item A uniform twist rotates $(E_{\ell m},B_{\ell m})$ by $2\beta$ and gives $C^{EE\prime}=\cos^22\beta\,C^{EE}+\sin^22\beta\,C^{BB}$, $C^{BB\prime}=\sin^22\beta\,C^{EE}+\cos^22\beta\,C^{BB}$, $C^{EB\prime}=\frac12\sin4\beta(C^{EE}-C^{BB})$, $C^{TE\prime}=\cos2\beta\,C^{TE}$, $C^{TB\prime}=\sin2\beta\,C^{TE}$; total polarized power is conserved.
\item Lensing also turns $E$ into $B$, $C^{BB}=\int\frac{\dd^2\ell'}{(2\pi)^2}[\bm\ell'\cdot(\bm\ell-\bm\ell')]^2\sin^22\Delta\varphi\,C^{\phi\phi}C^{EE}$, white at low $\ell$ ($\approx\TOneCLensWhiteUKarcmin\,\mu$K-arcmin), but leaves $EB=TB=0$: no hand.
\item Fingerprints of $B$: frequency separates the Galaxy and Faraday rotation from the CMB; the $\ell$ shape separates gravitational waves from lensing; $EB$, $TB$ separate twists from parity-preserving sources.
\item A detector angle error $\alpha$ adds to $\beta$; the Galaxy is twisted by $\alpha$ only, which gives the Minami--Komatsu relation \eqref{T1c:eq:mk-final}; dust $EB$ shifts the result to $\beta-\gamma$.
\item Measured: $\beta=0.35^\circ\pm0.14^\circ$ (Planck 2018), $0.30^\circ\pm0.11^\circ$ (PR4), $0.342^\circ{}^{+0.094^\circ}_{-0.091^\circ}$ (WMAP + Planck); not yet a detection because of the dust $EB$ and instrumental systematics.
\end{itemize}
\end{recap}
\section*{Chapter summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Chapter summary}
\label{T1a:sec:summary}

The cosmological model is the first step of the book's pipeline. For every choice of the six $\Lambda$CDM numbers it fixes the expected value of each summary that the later chapters estimate from data, and never the sky itself. The route is short enough to hold in the head. The Friedmann equation gives the expansion rate $E(z)$, and one integral of $1/E$ gives the distances $D_A$ and $D_L$. The primordial spectrum $\mathcal P_\zeta=A_s(k/k_*)^{n_s-1}$ is carried to the sky by the radiation transfer function, $C_\ell=\frac2\pi\int k^2\dd k\,P_\zeta\,g_{T\ell}^2$, and to the matter field by growth and the matter transfer function, $P_\delta(k,a_0)=P_\delta(k,a_{\mathrm i})\,T^2[D_+(a_0)/D_+(a_{\mathrm i})]^2$. The data are one draw from each: the $\alm$ with variance $\Cell$, the Fourier modes $\delta_{\bm k}$ with variance $P_\delta(k)$. Weak lensing adds a third field built from the same matter: projected along the line of sight with the lensing efficiency $g(\chi)$, it becomes the convergence $\kappa$, whose power spectrum $C_\ell^{\kappa\kappa}$ is a Limber integral of $P_\delta$, and it is read off galaxy shapes through the shear $\gamma$, buried under shape noise. The CMB light is also polarized, a field of headless sticks split into a mirror-symmetric $E$ and a mirror-odd $B$: ordinary physics leaves $EB=TB=0$, gravitational waves and lensing make $B$ without a hand, and a uniform twist of the polarization, by new physics ($\beta$) or by a miscalibrated detector ($\alpha$), moves power from $E$ to $B$ and creates $EB$ and $TB$.

What carries into the inference chapters is how these predictions move when the parameters move. The finite-difference responses $R_i(\ell)$ of \cref{T1a:eq:response} are the derivatives of the Fisher matrix, two nearly parallel responses are a degeneracy, and the line between linear and halofit $P(k)$ is where the Gaussian description of the matter field stops. Conventions are the fixed factors that decide whether a script reproduces these numbers. The table lists what to carry forward, what each item means in practice, and where it is used.

\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.3cm}
                               >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
                               >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
concept & operational meaning & used later in\\\midrule
model as a machine & six numbers into CAMB, expected statistics out; call it through \pyname{code/camb_fiducial.py} for the fiducial spectra & \cref{ch:07,ch:08,ch:09,ch:10}\\
one realisation & the sky is one draw; each $\alm$ has variance $\Cell$, and $\widehat C_\ell$ scatters about $\Cell$ as $\chi^2_{2\ell+1}/(2\ell+1)$ & \cref{ch:07,ch:08}\\
distances and $H(z)$ & $E(z)$ from the density parameters, then $D_A$, $D_L$ by one integral; the model mean of supernova and BAO data & \cref{ch:03,ch:05}\\
conventions & raw $\Cell$ vs $D_\ell$; $\mu\mathrm K^2$; $A_s=\frac{25}{4}A_S^2$; $h^{-1}$Mpc; $\phi\equiv\psi$ & every CMB and LSS script\\

$\xi(r)$ and the BAO bump & Fourier partner of $P(k)$; a peak near $r_s(z_{\mathrm{drag}})$, a standard ruler & \cref{ch:07,ch:12}\\
$\sigma_8$ & top-hat variance of the linear field at $8\,h^{-1}$Mpc; a check that $P(k)$ is normalised correctly & \cref{ch:07,ch:10}\\

$S_8$ and nuisances & cosmic shear fixes $\sigma_8\Omega_m^{\alpha}$, $\alpha\approx0.55$; photo-$z$, shear bias, intrinsic alignment, baryons are marginalised & \cref{ch:09,ch:10,ch:12}\\
$E$, $B$ and parity & $Q,U$ read along each wavevector; $B$ changes sign in a mirror, so $EB=TB=0$ unless something picks a hand (\cref{T1c:sec:parity}) & \cref{ch:13,ch:T3}\\
rotated spectra & a twist $\beta$ gives $EB=\frac12\sin4\beta\,(C^{EE}-C^{BB})$, $TB=\sin2\beta\,C^{TE}$, $BB$ gains $\sin^22\beta\,C^{EE}$; a detector angle error $\alpha$ enters as $\alpha+\beta$ & \cref{ch:13}\\

$B$-mode fingerprints & frequency (Galaxy vs CMB), $\ell$ shape (gravitational waves vs lensing), parity ($EB$, $TB$: twists vs the rest); table in \cref{T1c:sec:table} & \cref{ch:13}\\
Minami--Komatsu relation & the Galaxy is twisted by $\alpha$ only: $C^{EB,\mathrm o}=\frac12\tan4\alpha\,(C^{EE,\mathrm o}-C^{BB,\mathrm o})+\frac{\sin4\beta}{2\cos4\alpha}(C^{EE}-C^{BB})^{\mathrm{CMB}}$; dust $EB$ biases $\beta$ to $\beta-\gamma$ & \cref{ch:13}\\

\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Reading the literature.}
The physics of every object above is developed in the cosmology notes. The Boltzmann code used throughout is CAMB \cite{CAMB2000}. The parameter values and errors are those of Planck 2018 \cite{Planck2018VI}. The non-linear correction is the halofit version of \cite{Takahashi2012}, and the first detection of the BAO bump in the galaxy correlation function is \cite{Eisenstein2005}. For weak lensing, the standard reviews are \cite{BartelmannSchneider2001} and \cite{Kilbinger2015}, CMB lensing is reviewed in \cite{LewisChallinor2006} and measured in \cite{Planck2018VIII}, and the Stage-III cosmic-shear results are \cite{Asgari2021KiDS,Amon2022DES,Hikage2019HSC}. For CMB polarization, the $E$/$B$ decomposition is due to \cite{ZaldarriagaSeljak1997,Kamionkowski1997}, with a gentle introduction in \cite{HuWhite1997Pol}; gravitational-wave $B$ modes are predicted in \cite{SeljakZaldarriaga1997}, lensing $B$ modes in \cite{ZaldarriagaSeljak1998}, and experimental systematics are catalogued in \cite{HuHedmanZaldarriaga2003}. The physics of cosmic birefringence is in \cite{CarrollFieldJackiw1990,Carroll1998,LueWangKamionkowski1999}; the method that uses the Galaxy to calibrate the detector angles is \cite{Minami2019}, applied in \cite{MinamiKomatsu2020,DiegoPalazuelos2022,EskiltKomatsu2022}, with the dust $EB$ caveat of \cite{Clark2021}. The polarized foregrounds are characterised in \cite{Planck2018XI} and the current limit on $r$ is \cite{BICEPKeck2021}.
